In addition to our VAE, we design a variant called VAE-D, where the\nreconstruction loss is replaced by a diffusion loss. We adopt both the pre-trained VAE and VAE-D on\nthe text-to-image generation task for 150,000 training steps until achieving convergence. We measure\n25\n\n\n--- PAGE 26 ---\nFigure 17: Training loss curve with different text encoders.\nthe FID scores at both 100,000 and 150,000 steps. As shown in Table 5, VAE model consistently\nachieves lower FID compared to VAE-D.\n5\nEXTENDED APPLICATIONS\n5.1\nIMAGE-TO-VIDEO GENERATION\nThe image-to-video (I2V) generation task focuses on synthesizing a dynamic video sequence from a\nstatic input image guided by a textual prompt. This approach significantly enhances the controllability\nof video generation by anchoring the output to a specific initial frame, thereby garnering substantial\ninterest within the research community. Existing approaches, such as I2VGen-XL (Zhang et al.,\n2023c), SVD (Blattmann et al., 2023), and CogVideo (Yang et al., 2025b), extend T2V frameworks\nto I2V by channel-wise concatenation of the conditional latent representation with the noise latent.\nBuilding upon this paradigm, our Wan-I2V model adopts a similar strategy, capitalizing on the robust\nprior knowledge embedded in our foundational T2V model to achieve high-quality I2V generation.\n5.1.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nWe introduce an additional condition image as the first frame to control video synthesizing. Specif-\nically, we concatenate the condition image I ∈RC×1×H×W with zero-filled frames along the\ntemporal axis, and these guidance frames Ic ∈RC×T ×H×W are compressed by Wan-VAE into\ncondition latent zc ∈Rc×t×h×w (c = 16 denotes the latent channel, t = 1 + (T −1)/4, h = H/8,\nw = W/8). Additionally, we introduce a binary mask M ∈{0, 1}1×T ×h×w where 1 indicates the\npreserved frame and 0 denotes the frames to be generated. The spatial size of mask M is consistent\nwith condition latent zc, but M shares the same temporal length with the target video, which is then\nrearranged as shape of s × t × h × w where s is temporal stride of Wan-VAE. The noise latent zt,\ncondition latent zc, and rearranged mask m are concatenated along the channel axis and then passed\nthrough the DiT model of Wan. Since the input of the I2V DiT model has more channels than T2V\n(i.e., 2 × c + s v.s. c), an additional projection layer is employed, which is initialized with zero\nvalues. Moreover, we utilize the image encoder of CLIP (Radford et al., 2021) to extract feature\nrepresentations from the condition image, and the extracted features are projected by a three-layer\nmulti-layer perceptron (MLP), which plays a role as global context. The produced global context is\nthen injected into the DiT model via decoupled cross-attention.\nIn practice, the aforementioned approach as illustrated in Fig. 18 is extensible to other controllable\ngeneration tasks, such as first-last frame transformation and video continuation. The proposed\nmasking mechanism explicitly delineates the input conditions and specifies the frames to be generated.\nTo optimize the efficacy of the masking strategy, we incorporate multiple tasks, i.e., image-to-video\ngeneration, video continuation, first-last frame transformation, and random frame interpolation, into\nour unified framework. Specifically, we adopt the following training paradigm for the I2V model.\n26\n\n\n--- PAGE 27 ---\nWan-\nEncoder\nN × DiT Blocks\numT5\nEncoder\n“A woman in a \nwhite spin is \ndoing yoga on a \nsoccer field”\nWan-\nDecoder\nFirst\nframe\nlatent\nmask\nOutput video\nDiffusion Process\nTimestep t\nCLIP\nImage Encoder\nDecoupled \nCross-Attention\nX𝑡\nFigure 18: Wan-I2V model framework. The mask mechanism enables the model to selectively focus\non input frames such that the proposed framework demonstrates compatibility with video continuation\nand first-last frame transformation tasks.\nDuring the joint training phase, we conduct unified pre-training of our mask-guided model across\nthese diverse tasks. This phase facilitates the model’s ability to discern which positions should be\npreserved and which should be generated, depending on the input content. Subsequently, in the\nfine-tuning stage, we concentrate on refining the model’s performance on each individual task.\n5.1.2\nDATASET\nDuring the joint training phase, we utilize the same training dataset employed for T2V pertaining.\nThis phase equips the model with the foundational capability to generate motion conditioned on\nreference images. In the fine-tuning phase, we curate task-specific datasets tailored to the unique\ncharacteristics of each individual task.\nImage-to-video dataset. In the early experiments, we found that when the first frame in the training\ndata significantly differed from the video content, the model struggled to learn stable image-driven\nvideo generation. From this perspective, a smaller difference between the first frame and the video\ncontent in the training data is beneficial for model training. We calculate the difference between\nthe first frame and the video content based on SigLIP (Zhai et al., 2023) features. Specifically, we\ncompute the cosine similarity between the features of the first frame and the mean of the features of\nthe remaining frames, and then we retain only the videos with a similarity greater than a predefined\nthreshold.\nVideo continuation dataset. Similarly, our findings also indicate that training on temporally\nconsistent videos significantly improves the performance of video continuation tasks. To quantify the\nconsistency between the initial and final segments of a video (i.e., the first 1.5 seconds and the last\n3.5 seconds), we compute the cosine similarity of their respective SigLIP features, followed by using\nthe computed similarity scores to filter and select the required training dataset.\nFirst-last frame transformation dataset. Unlike the I2V task, the community places greater\nemphasis on the smooth transition between the initial and final frames in the mage transition task. To\nthis end, we increase the proportion of data samples with significant transition between the first and\nlast frames in the training dataset.\n5.1.3\nEVALUATION\nThe training of the I2V model, along with first-last frame transformation and video continuation,\nis structured into two stages. In the initial pre-training stage, we employ the same dataset as that\nused in the T2V task. Given the slightly larger number of frames involved in this stage, the image\nencoder branch is excluded, which is motivated by the need to maintain a strong alignment between\ntextual input and visual output, ensuring that the model retains its sensitivity to nuanced textual\nsemantics. Upon the stabilization of the pre-training stage, the model has already manifested a variety\nof capabilities, including I2V generation, video continuation, and so on. This demonstrates the\nefficacy of the pre-training process in establishing a robust foundation for both spatial and temporal\nunderstanding.\n27\n\n\n--- PAGE 28 ---\nFigure 19: Videos generated by Wan-I2V model. The results demonstrate that our I2V model\neffectively captures and replicates the dynamics of real-world scenes.\n28\n\n\n--- PAGE 29 ---\nFigure 20: Videos generated by Wan-I2V model. The results indicate that our I2V model can\neffectively animate diverse types of images into highly realistic and dynamic videos.\n29\n\n\n--- PAGE 30 ---\nCN-TopA\nCN-TopB\nCN-TopC\nCN-TopD\nAll Rounds\nVisual Quality\n29.2%\n60.8%\n24.6%\n55.6%\n896\nMotion Quality\n21.7%\n21.7%\n32.5%\n67.0%\n890\nMatching\n-4.2%\n35.0%\n51.7%\n72.2%\n896\nOverall Ranking\n10.8%\n47.5%\n50.8%\n81.6%\n890\nTable 7: Win rate gap of I2V models. The values in the table represent the proportion of instances in\nwhich the Wan-I2V model was preferred in pairwise comparisons against other models, relative to\nthe total number of comparisons conducted.\nOur empirical observations reveal that relying solely on frame-level information is inadequate\nfor achieving optimal performance in specific tasks, such as image-to-video generation, video\ncontinuation, and first-last frame transformation.\nThis insight indicates that while the pre-trained model offers a foundational framework for frame-\nbased video generation, it lacks the requisite contextual and semantic depth to fully capture the\ntemporal consistency of dynamic video sequences, particularly when the number of conditioning\nframes is highly limited.\nTo address this limitation, during the SFT stage, we incorporated an image encoder to extract image\nfeatures through decoupled cross-attention (Ye et al., 2023) like (Guo et al., 2024a), providing global\ncontextual information to enhance the model’s capacities. We conducted experiments based on\ntwo pre-trained checkpoints at 480p and 720p resolutions, covering the training of image-to-video\ngeneration, video continuation, and first-last frame transformation tasks.\nSimilarly to human evaluation for our T2V model, we benchmark our I2V model against state-of-the-\nart techniques to provide a comprehensive comparison focusing on key aspects such as visual quality,\nmotion quality, and matching. The results shown as Table 7 demonstrate that our model performs\nfavorably across all dimensions evaluated. Fig. 19 and Fig. 20 present additional visualizations of\nour I2V model, offering a clear illustration of its superior performance in animating images into\nhigh-quality videos.\n5.2\nUNIFIED VIDEO EDITING\nThe foundational pre-trained models for text-to-image and text-to-video generation have facilitated\nthe expansion of various downstream tasks and applications, including repainting (AI, 2022; Zhou\net al., 2023), editing (Meng et al., 2021; Brooks et al., 2023; Zhang et al., 2023a; Wang et al., 2023b;\nWei et al., 2024b), controllable generation (Zhang et al., 2023b; Jiang et al., 2024; Wang et al., 2024c),\ncustomized generation (Chen et al., 2023b; Wei et al., 2025), frame reference generation (Yang et al.,\n2025b; Guo et al., 2024a), efficient generation (Wang et al., 2023c; Yuan et al., 2024a; Liu et al.,\n2024b), and ID-referenced video synthesis (Pan et al., 2024; Yuan et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2025a).\nTo enhance task flexibility and minimize the overhead associated with deploying multiple models,\nresearchers have increasingly focused on developing unified model architectures (Tan et al., 2024;\nChen et al., 2024), such as ACE (Han et al., 2025; Mao et al., 2025) and OmniGen (Xiao et al.,\n2024). These unified architectures aim to integrate diverse tasks within a single image model, thereby\nfacilitating the creation of various application workflows while maintaining ease of use. In the domain\nof video, the collaborative transformations in both temporal and spatial dimensions suggest that\nleveraging a unified model can unlock limitless possibilities for video creation.\nIn our previous work, VACE (Jiang et al., 2025), we introduced a unified framework for controllable\nvideo generation and editing, addressing tasks such as reference-to-video generation, video-to-video\nediting, and masked video-to-video editing. Building upon the pre-trained model of Wan, we integrate\nmultiple modalities, including images and videos for editing, references, and masks, into the Video\nCondition Unit (VCU) to support diverse input formats. VACE employs a concept decoupling strategy\nthat enables the model to identify which aspects should be retained and which should be modified,\n30\n\n\n--- PAGE 31 ---\nFigure 21: Unified controllable generation and editing framework. Frames and masks are tokenized\nthrough Concept Decoupling, Context Latent Encode, and Context Embedder. We propose two\ntraining strategies for Wan: Fully Fine-tuning and Context Adapter Tuning.\nthereby distinctly separating visual modality information in editing and reference tasks. We offer two\ntraining modes. The first mode involves training with the VCU as input, fully fine-tuning the entire\nWan model. The second mode utilizes a pluggable Context Adapter structure in a Res-Tuning (Jiang\net al., 2023) manner, allowing support for controllable and editing tasks without modifying the base\nmodel’s weights but with an increased overall model scale. Leveraging the robust video generation\ncapabilities of Wan, this innovative framework demonstrates significant competitiveness in both\nquantitative and qualitative evaluations. It facilitates the compositional expansion of fundamental\ntasks, enabling scenarios such as long video re-rendering. Consequently, the framework offers a\nversatile and efficient solution for video synthesis, opening new avenues for user-driven video content\ncreation and editing.\n5.2.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nThe Video Condition Unit (VCU), proposed in VACE, serves as an input paradigm that unifies\ndiverse input conditions into textual inputs, frame sequences, and mask sequences. It can be formally\nrepresented as follows:\nV = [T; F; M],\n(4)\nwhere T is a text prompt, while F and M are sequences of context video frames {u1, u2, ..., un}\nand masks {m1, m2, ..., mn} respectively. Here, u is in RGB space, normalized to [−1, 1] and m is\nbinary, in which “1”s and “0”s symbolize where to edit or not. F and M are aligned both in spatial\nsize h × w and temporal size n. Under this paradigm, we employ the previously described Wan-VAE\nto tokenize the input video frames and mask information into context tokens. As illustrated in Fig. 21\n(a), these context tokens are then combined with noisy video tokens to fine-tune the Wan model.\nAdditionally, a Context Adapter Tuning strategy is proposed, which allows context tokens to pass\nthrough Context Blocks and be reintegrated into the original DiT blocks. This approach facilitates\nthe seamless integration of contextual information, enhancing the model’s ability to handle diverse\nediting and generation tasks without altering the base model’s weights.\n31\n\n\n--- PAGE 32 ---\nTASK-Outpainting: A large spaceship is flying through space, with a smaller ship visible in the \nbackground. To develop an all-in-one model, the diversity and complexity of data construction\nmust increase. For controllable video generation and editing tasks, input modalities are expanded\nto include target videos, source videos, local masks, references, and more. Efficient and rapid data\nacquisition for various tasks necessitates maintaining video quality while performing instance-level\nanalysis and understanding. We begin by performing shot slicing on the video data and initially\nfiltering based on resolution, aesthetic score, and motion amplitude. The first frame is then labeled\nusing RAM (Zhang et al., 2023d) and processed with Grounding DINO (Liu et al., 2023d) for\ndetection, enabling secondary filtering of videos with target areas that are either too small or too large.\nAdditionally, we utilize the propagation operation of SAM2 (Ravi et al., 2025) for video segmentation\nto obtain instance-level information throughout the video. Based on the segmentation results, we\nfurther filter instances temporally by calculating the effective frame ratio according to a mask area\nthreshold. The construction for different tasks must be tailored to each task’s specific characteristics.\nFor detailed information, refer to the work (Jiang et al., 2025).\nImplementation details. We train the controllable and editing model, pre-trained on Wan-T2V-14B,\nto support resolutions up to 720p through a multi-stage training process. Initially, we focus on\nfoundational tasks such as inpainting and extension to complement the pre-trained text-to-video\n34\n\n\n--- PAGE 35 ---\nmodels. This phase incorporates masks and develops contextual generation capabilities in both spatial\nand temporal dimensions. Subsequently, we expand the task by transitioning from single-input to\nmultiple-input reference frames and from individual to composite tasks. Finally, we enhance the\nmodel’s quality by fine-tuning it with higher-quality data and longer sequences.\n5.2.3\nEVALUATION\nIn Fig. 22 and Fig. 23, we present the results of the Wan single model across various tasks. It is\nevident that the model achieves a high level of performance in video quality and temporal consis-\ntency. Additionally, we demonstrate new applications resulting from the combination of different\ngenerative capabilities, as illustrated in Fig. 24 (a). Our model performs effectively on these tasks,\nshowcasing significant potential for capability expansion. The unified controllable generation and\nediting framework proposed by VACE is also applicable to image generation and editing, as shown in\nFig. 24 (b).\n5.3\nTEXT-TO-IMAGE GENERATION\nWan is jointly trained on both image and video datasets, equipping it with not only advanced video\ngeneration capabilities but also exceptional image synthesis performance. This dual-training strategy\nfacilitates cross-modal knowledge transfer, creating a highly versatile framework that achieves\npowerful results in both domains. In practice, our model has been trained on an image dataset nearly\nten times larger than its video counterpart, allowing for synergistic benefits between image and video\ngeneration tasks. This extensive training regimen has led to outstanding performance in various\naspects of image synthesis. As demonstrated in Fig. 25, Wan generates high-fidelity images across\ndiverse categories, encompassing artistic text-based visuals, photorealistic portraits, imaginative\ncreative designs, and professional-grade product photography.\n5.4\nVIDEO PERSONALIZATION\nVideo personalization aims to generate videos that maintain consistent identity with the user-provided\nreference (Wei et al., 2024a; Li et al., 2024; Yuan et al., 2025). In this section, we integrate advanced\npersonalization techniques into the video generation pipeline, achieving state-of-the-art performance.\nThe subsequent discussion provides a comprehensive elaboration of our approach, encompassing the\nmodel architecture, personalization data, and experimental validation.\n5.4.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nThe core techniques behind video personalization tackle two primary challenges: (1) the acquisition\nof high-fidelity personalized identities and (2) the seamless integration of these identity features into\nthe video generation pipeline.\nWe start from our Wan-T2V foundation model by first obtaining personalized identity information.\nExisting video personalization approaches usually rely on ID extractors (e.g., ArcFace (Deng et al.,\n2019)) or versatile visual extractors (e.g., CLIP (Radford et al., 2021)) to obtain identity information.\nWhile they make promising progress, their performance is bottlenecked by the limitations of the\nfeature extractors. For instance, ID extractors mainly focus on features for face recognition but may\nnot capture other critical visual cues such as scars or stickers. Moreover, they are susceptible to\nlow-resolution faces, illumination variations, and partial occlusions (such as glasses, masks, or hair).\nOn the other hand, versatile visual extractors are not specifically tailored to the face domain and tend\nto focus on coarse-grained semantic information. Therefore, we choose not to rely on any feature\nextractor to avoid information loss, and our generation process is directly conditioned on the input\nface image in the latent space of Wan-VAE.\nThere are many ways to inject the personalized identity information into the video generation\nprocess. In our experiments, we found that cross-attention operation is suited to interact with\ndense representations obtained from identity extractors, while self-attention is more appropriate for\nmodeling data in the same latent space. Fig. 26 illustrates the design of our video personalization\napproach, which follows a self-attention paradigm. Specifically, in the latent space, we first extend K\nadditional frames prepended to the given video using the segmented face images. These face images\nare extracted from the paired video using face detection and segmentation, and the facial landmarks\n35\n\n\n--- PAGE 36 ---\nFigure 25: Image samples generated from Wan model.\n36\n\n\n--- PAGE 37 ---\nChannel dimension\nCondition\nsignals\nExtended\nvideo\nK extended frames\nVideo frames\nAll-ones mask\nAll-ones mask\nAll-zeros mask\nAll-zeros mask\nAll-zeros mask\nFigure 26: The core design of the video personalization approach in the latent space of Wan-VAE.\nare further aligned to a black canvas that has the same size as the video frame. Then, along the\nchannel dimension of this extended video, we concatenate the face images with all-ones masks for the\nfirst K frames, and blank images with all-zeros masks for the remaining frames. These concatenated\nsignals serve as the conditions. Finally, the diffusion process is performed on this temporally extended\nvideo, conditioned on the channel-wise condition signals in an inpainting fashion. Note that no other\nmodifications are applied to our Wan-T2V model.\nDuring training, we randomly drop a portion of face images in the K extended frames, so as to\nsupport 0 to K reference face(s) video generation. In the inference stage, starting from the pure\nnoise of the extended video, we concatenate the user-provided face images (no more than K) to the\nextended frames in the channel dimension and set the corresponding masks as all-ones. The goal is\nto reconstruct the face images in the first K frames, and synthesize a new personalized video in the\nsubsequent frames that preserves the provided identity.\n5.4.2\nDATASET\nWe curate a collection of personalization data from our T2V foundation model’s training dataset\nthrough careful filtering and automatic data synthesizing. We first filter out about O(100)M videos\nusing our internal human classifiers and perform face detection at 1 FPS on these videos. A video\nshall be discarded if more than one face is detected in any frame or if over 10% of the frames have\nno face detected. Then we calculate ArcFace similarities between consecutive frames and discard\nvideos with low similarities. Afterwards, we perform face segmentation to remove the distracting\nbackgrounds, and perform face landmark detection so as to facilitate canvas alignment during our\ntraining. Note that we do not filter out faces with the small areas, because we find that such videos\nusually contain full-body human figures. Finally, we construct about O(10)M personalized videos,\nwhere each video is accompanied by 5 segmented faces in average.\nWe also resort to automatic data synthesizing to improve facial diversity. Particularly, we randomly\nselect O(1)M personalized videos from above, and adopt Instant-ID (Wang et al., 2024b) to synthesize\ndiverse faces for each video. Notably, we build a text template with over 100 prompts, including\nanime, line art, cinematic, Minecraft, and so on. Every time we take a random prompt from this\ntemplate, as well as a random human pose estimated from the rest of the videos, to serve as inputs\nto Instant-ID. We further measure the ArcFace similarity on the generated faces and filter out those\nwith lower similarities. In total, we gather about O(1)M videos with synthesized faces, which greatly\nimprove the diversity of styles, poses, illuminations, and occlusions on our personalization dataset.\nWan\nCN-TopA\nCN-TopB\nCN-TopC\nArcface Similarity\n0.5526\n0.5655\n0.5197\n0.4998\nTable 8: Comparison of other leading video personalization methods.\n5.4.3\nEVALUATION\nWe present our video personalization results in Fig. 27, where the left part represents the input\nidentities and the right part represents the synthesized personalized videos. All the testing images are\n37\n\n\n--- PAGE 38 ---\nrandomly picked from Pexels1. We also evaluate our model on an unseen evaluation set and measure\nthe ArcFace similarity between input faces and output personalized videos by sampling faces at 1 FPS\nfrom videos. The final similarity scores are averaged from these sampled faces. The corresponding\nresults are illustrated in Table 8, where our approach demonstrates competitive video personalization\nperformance compared to other leading commercial and closed-source Chinese competitors.\n5.5\nCAMERA MOTION CONTROLLABILITY\nThe camera motion control module is designed to accurately match the motion and viewpoint of the\nvideo by leveraging camera trajectory. Specifically, we utilize the extrinsic parameters [R, t] ∈R3×4\nand intrinsic parameters Kf ∈R3×3 for each frame. Our approach consists of two main components\nto effectively inject camera motion conditions: camera pose encoder and camera pose adapter, as\nillustrated in Fig. 28.\nCamera pose encoder. First, for each pixel in each video frame, we use Pl¨ucker coordinates to\ntransform the given extrinsic and intrinsic parameters into a sequence of fine-grained positions\nP ∈R6×F ×H×W . To adjust the compression resolution of video latent features, we apply the\nPixelUnshuffle operation in Pytorch (Paszke et al., 2019) to reduce the spatial resolution of P while\nincreasing the number of channels. Finally, we encode the output using a series of convolutional\nmodules aligned with the number of DiT blocks to extract multi-level camera motion features.\nCamera pose adapter. To integrate camera motion features into video latent features, we adopt an\nadaptive normalization layer. Specifically, we transform the input camera motion feature sequence\ninto scaling factors γi and shifting parameters βi using two zero-initialized convolution layers. Then,\nγi and βi are incorporated into each DiT block through a simple linear projection defined by the\nformula fi = (γi + 1) ∗fi−1 + βi, where fi represents the video latent features at layer i.\nRegarding the training data, we utilize an advanced camera pose estimation algorithm named VGG-\nSfM (Wang et al., 2024a) to extract camera trajectories from our training videos that exhibit significant\ncamera motion. This process yields approximately O(1) thousand video segments. We train our\nmodule within our text-to-video generation framework using the Adam optimizer. Fig. 29 presents\nvarious example results of videos generated under camera motion guidance.\n5.6\nREAL-TIME VIDEO GENERATION\nCurrent video generative methods often require significant computational resources and considerable\ntime to produce even short video clips. Systems relying on high-end hardware remain slow, often\ntaking several minutes to generate just a few seconds of video. While the visual quality of these\ngenerated videos has seen substantial improvements in recent years, the extended processing times\npresent a major bottleneck in practical applications and iterative design workflows. In fields where\nrapid prototyping and real-time adjustments are crucial—such as interactive entertainment, virtual\nreality, and video production—these long generation times limit creative flexibility and slow down\nthe process of refining visual content.\nMoreover, the slow speed of current methods limits their use in dynamic environments where real-\ntime feedback is essential. In contexts like live-streaming or gaming, there’s an increasing demand\nfor systems that can instantly render synthetic scenes and characters in response to user actions or\nchanging inputs. Any delay in generating high-quality video clips can result in a suboptimal user\nexperience, reducing immersion and interactivity. By focusing on real-time generation, Wan seeks\nto enhance both the performance and applicability of video generation techniques, broadening their\npotential in fast-paced, interactive environments. Fig. 30 and 31 provide such cases of real-time long\nvideo stream generation. We then introduce how to achieve this using our Wan models.\n5.6.1\nMETHOD\nTo construct the real-time generation pipeline, we build upon a previously pre-trained version of the\nWan model. This design choice offers two primary benefits. First, starting from a well-trained model\nsignificantly accelerates convergence and improves training stability, as the model already possesses\na strong initialization. Second, the pre-trained model has already captured valuable knowledge about\n1www.pexels.com\n38\n\n\n--- PAGE 39 ---\nFigure 27: Visualization of our video personalization approach.\n39\n\n\n--- PAGE 40 ---\nA\npanda\nis holding up a\ncardboard that says “Wan2.1”.\nSelf Attention\nCross Attention\nFFN\nCamera Adapter\n…\nRT = {rt0, rt1, … rtT}\nN×DiT Blocks\nWan-\nEncoder\nContent \nEmbedding\nCamera Adapter\nCamera \nEmbedding\n[B, T, H, W, C’]\nScale, Shift\nResBlock\n[B, T, H, W, C]\nReshape\n[ γ, β ]\nContent \nEmbedding\n[B, T*H*W, C]\nReshape\nContent \nEmbedding\n[B, T*H*W, C]\nCamera Pose\n[B, T, 12]\nCamera Encoder\nPlücker Embedding\n[B, T, H’, W’, 6]\nResBlock\nResBlock\n+\n+\n*N\n[B, T, H, W, C’]\nCamera \nEncoder\nText\nEncoder\nFigure 28: Framework of video generation guided by camera motion.\nmotion patterns and continuous temporal dynamics, which can be directly inherited by the new\npipeline to enhance the smoothness and coherence of generated content. Therefore, we adopt the\npre-trained Wan model as the foundation for our real-time pipeline 2.\nThe pre-trained Wan model is designed to generate fixed-length video clips, typically 5 to 10 seconds\nlong. To transform this into a real-time streaming model capable of generating continuous video\nstreams with no predefined length (potentially infinite), two key modifications are required:\n• Streaming pipeline adaptation. We replace the static generation process, which produces\na fixed-length sequence of video tokens in a single pass, with a streaming mechanism that\nincrementally generates video tokens. In this streaming setup, video tokens are processed\nthrough a denoising queue — each time the oldest token is generated and dequeued, a new\ntoken is added to the bottom of the queue, enabling continuous generation without length\nconstraints.\n• Real-time acceleration. In addition to enabling streaming, we optimize the generation\nspeed to ensure the system meets real-time performance requirements, i.e., the pipeline must\ngenerate new frames fast enough to keep up with real-time playback.\nIn the following sections, we will describe each of these modifications in detail, including their design\nrationale, technical implementation, and empirical performance.\n5.6.2\nSTREAMING VIDEO GENERATION\nTraditional Diffusion Transformer (DiT) (Peebles & Xie, 2023) models are constrained in their\nability to generate videos longer than a few seconds, even when employing significant spatial and\n2Part of this section is introduced in a previously released tech report The Matrix (Feng et al., 2024).\n40\n\n\n--- PAGE 41 ---\nFigure 29: Generated video results guided by camera motion.\ntemporal compression through Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) (Kingma, 2013). This limitation\nstems primarily from the computational and memory-intensive nature of attention mechanisms over\nextended temporal sequences. To overcome this challenge, we introduce Streamer, a novel approach\nthat leverages a sliding temporal window to manage temporal dependencies efficiently, enabling the\ngeneration of long or even infinite-length videos in a streaming fashion.\n41\n\n\n--- PAGE 42 ---\nFigure 30: Generated 15 minutes video with 8 A100 GPUs in real-time 8 FPS.\nShift window denoise process models.\nThe key innovation of Streamer lies in its assumption that\ntemporal dependencies are confined within a limited time window. By focusing attention computations\nwithin this window, Streamer significantly reduces computational overhead while maintaining video\ncontinuity. Specifically, Streamer processes video tokens in a queue, denoising them over multiple\n42\n\n\n--- PAGE 43 ---\nFigure 31: Generated videos with a single RTX 4090 GPU, int8 and TensorRT quantization in\nreal-time 20 FPS.\nnoise levels within the window. After a fixed number of denoising steps, the leftmost token (with\nthe lowest noise level) is dequeued and cached, while a new token with Gaussian noise is appended\nto the rightmost position. This sliding mechanism ensures that the window maintains a consistent\nlength while facilitating the generation of continuous video frames.\nTraining and inference.\nStreamer is fine-tuned from a pre-trained DiT model. During training, a\nsequence of 2w video tokens is sampled, where w is the size of the sliding window (typically set to\nw = T, the number of diffusion solver steps). The first w tokens are used solely for ‘warming up’ the\nmodel and do not contribute to the loss computation. The loss is calculated only on the last w tokens,\nensuring that the model learns to generate coherent video frames within the window. At inference\ntime, the same warmup strategy is employed: the first w tokens are discarded, and the generated\nvideo begins from the (w + 1)-th token.\nEnsuring continuity during training and inference.\nTo maintain smooth transitions between\nconsecutive windows, cached tokens are re-introduced into the token queue at a noise level of 0. This\nallows previously generated tokens to participate in the denoising process of subsequent windows,\nensuring temporal consistency and continuity in the generated video.\nStreamer represents a significant advancement in video generation by adapting DiT models for\nstreaming applications. Its sliding temporal window approach addresses the limitations of traditional\nDiT models, enabling the efficient generation of infinite-length videos while maintaining high quality\nand temporal coherence. This method opens new possibilities for real-time video generation and\nlong-form content creation. We summarize its advantages as follows:\n• Infinite-Length Video Generation: By leveraging a sliding window mechanism, Streamer\ncan generate videos of arbitrary length without incurring prohibitive computational costs;\n• Efficient Attention Computation: Limiting attention computations to a confined temporal\nwindow reduces memory and processing demands;\n43\n\n\n--- PAGE 44 ---\n• Seamless Continuity: The caching and re-introduction of tokens ensure smooth transitions\nbetween windows, preserving video quality over extended durations.\n5.6.3\nCONSISTENCY MODEL DISTILLATION\nAfter successfully extending the DiT model to Streamer for infinite-length video generation, the next\ncritical step is to address the challenge of achieving real-time rendering of the simulated world. While\nStreamer enables the generation of long or even infinite videos by efficiently managing temporal\ndependencies within a sliding window, the computational demands of the diffusion process still\npose a bottleneck for real-time applications. To overcome this, we propose a novel integration\nof Streamer with Consistency Models, a cutting-edge approach for accelerating diffusion-based\ngenerative processes. Specifically, we leverage the Latent Consistency Model (LCM (Luo et al.,\n2023)) and its video version (VideoLCM (Wang et al., 2023c)), which distill the original diffusion\nprocess and class-free guidance into a highly efficient four-step consistency model. This distillation\nprocess significantly reduces the number of sampling steps required for high-quality generation while\nmaintaining the temporal coherence and streaming capabilities inherent to Streamer.\nThe integration of LCM with Streamer is designed to preserve the denoising window mechanism,\nensuring that the sliding temporal window continues to manage dependencies effectively. During\ntraining, we optimize this combined framework to balance efficiency and quality. The result is a\n10 - 20 × acceleration in inference speed, enabling the model to achieve a rendering rate of 8 - 16\nFPS. This dramatic improvement in performance makes the system viable for real-time applications,\nsuch as interactive simulations, live video synthesis, and dynamic content generation in virtual\nenvironments. By combining the strengths of Streamer—its ability to generate infinite-length videos\nwith temporal coherence—and LCM—its efficiency in accelerating the diffusion process—we bridge\nthe gap between high-quality video generation and real-time responsiveness. This advancement not\nonly enhances the practicality of diffusion models for streaming applications but also opens up new\npossibilities for immersive, interactive, and real-time media creation.\nQuantization for running in customer-level devices.\nWhile the generating speed has been accel-\nerated to real-time levels, deploying the model on consumer-level devices remains challenging due to\nthe high computational and memory requirements, even for high-end GPUs like the NVIDIA 4090.\nTo address this, we introduce quantization techniques to optimize the model for efficient deployment.\nSpecifically, we employ two distinct quantization strategies: int8 quantization (torchao maintainers &\ncontributors, 2024) for attention layers and linear heads, and TensorRT quantization (NVIDIA, 2023)\nfor the overall model. The int8 quantization approach significantly reduces memory consumption by\nconverting weights and activations to 8-bit integers, while maintaining the overall generation quality.\nHowever, this method provides minimal acceleration in generating speed, as it primarily focuses on\nmemory efficiency rather than computational optimization. On the other hand, TensorRT quantization\noffers a more comprehensive solution, enabling substantial acceleration in generation speed. This\ntechnique allows the model to achieve real-time performance of 8 FPS even on a single 4090 GPU,\nmaking it feasible for consumer-level devices. However, TensorRT quantization introduces a modest\nincrease in network error, which can be observed even when its built-in error-checking mechanisms\nare enabled. This error manifests as slight deviations in output quality, such as minor artifacts or\ninconsistencies in the generated video. In some cases, it may also lead to measurable instability, such\nas flickering or temporal incoherence. Despite these trade-offs, the combination of int8 and TensorRT\nquantization provides a balanced approach to optimizing the model for real-time, consumer-level\ndeployment, ensuring both efficiency and practicality while maintaining acceptable generation quality.\nBy carefully tuning the quantization parameters and leveraging TensorRT’s error-checking features,\nwe mitigate these issues to the greatest extent possible, enabling robust and efficient performance on\nconsumer-grade hardware.\n5.7\nAUDIO GENERATION\nThe primary objective of audio generation in this work is to produce synchronized soundtracks for\nvideo clips, formulated as a video-to-audio (V2A) generation framework. The generated soundtracks\nconsist of ambient sound and background music, explicitly excluding speech or vocal elements.\nIn contrast to text-to-audio models (Liu et al., 2023b; 2024c), the ambient sound generated by\nvideo-to-audio models must be temporally aligned with the visual content of the video, while the\n44\n\n\n--- PAGE 45 ---\nOutput waveform\nDiT blocks × N\numT5\n……\n1D-VAE\n1D-VAE\nDecoder\nEncoder\nThe video description: a horse \nis running. This audio \ncontains ambient sound: the \nsound of clip-clop. This audio \ndoes not contain music.\nAudio waveform\nConditioning\nCLIP\nAdd noise\nTemporal Alignment \nFigure 32: Framework of video-to-audio generation. Our model processes both a video chunk and its\nassociated textual description as dual inputs to synthesize high-quality, semantically coherent audio.\naccompanying music should accurately reflect the emotional tone and contextual setting of the\nvideo, as would naturally be expected. Furthermore, to enhance user control over sound design,\nour framework integrates both video and textual prompts, enabling users to specify on-screen or\noff-screen sounds and define the style and presence of background music.\n5.7.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nConsistent with our video generation framework, our video-to-audio model also utilizes a diffusion\ntransformer (DiT) (Peebles & Xie, 2023) based on the flow-matching (Lipman et al., 2022) to model\nthe denoising diffusion process in the audio domain. The detailed pipeline of our V2A model is\nillustrated in Fig. 32.\nAudio autoencoder. A widely adopted audio compression approach involves transforming raw\nwaveforms into mel-spectrograms, followed by encoding them into Ha × W a × Ca latent features\nusing an image-based variational autoencoder, as demonstrated in (Liu et al., 2023b; 2024c). However,\nin the context of a diffusion transformer backbone, these latent features must be partitioned into\npatches and reshaped into a tensor of size (Ha · W a) × Ca. This patching-and-reshaping process can\ndisrupt the temporal alignment with the corresponding video content, posing a significant challenge\nfor synchronization. Considering this limitation, we train a compression model that operates directly\non raw waveforms. Our 1D-VAE generates latent features of size T a × Ca, where T a represents the\nsequence length along the time axis, preserving explicit temporal information essential for accurate\nsynchronization.\nVideo and text encoder. To achieve frame-accurate synchronization between synthesized audio\nand visual sequences, we establish temporal coherence through multimodal feature fusion. Our\narchitecture first extracts frame-wise visual embeddings using a CLIP Radford et al. (2021) model,\nthen performs temporal rate adaptation through feature replication to match the audio feature’s\nsampling rate, following (Polyak et al.). The synchronized visual and acoustic features undergo\ndimension transformation via linear projection layers to align with DiT’s latent space, followed by\nelement-wise summation for multimodal fusion. For linguistic understanding across languages, we\nleverage the pre-trained umT5 model (Chung et al., 2023) as our text encoder, capitalizing on its\ncross-lingual representation capabilities through frozen parameters.\nData. The training data for our V2A model is derived from the video generation dataset through\na rigorous filtering process. We systematically remove videos lacking soundtracks or containing\nspeech/vocal music, resulting in a refined subset of O(1) thousand hours. To enhance multimodal\nrepresentation, we employ a comprehensive captioning strategy: dense video descriptions are com-\nplemented with audio-specific captions generated by Qwen2-audio (Chu et al., 2024). These audio\ncaptions are categorized into ambient sounds and musical compositions, with the latter characterized\nby style, rhythm, melody, and instrumentation. The final structured caption integrates three compo-\nnents: (1) dense video description, (2) ambient sound characterization, and (3) background music\nanalysis, providing a unified multimodal representation for training.\n45\n\n\n--- PAGE 46 ---\nOurs\nMMAudio\nA person holds a coffee pot above a cup and slowly pours the steaming coffee into it.\nOurs\nMMAudio\nA person sits alone in a crowded café, typing on a laptop, with people chatting softly and moving quietly in the \nbackground.\nOurs\nMMAudio\nA nurse walked quietly down a dimly lit hospital corridor, passing by row upon row of empty rooms.\nOurs\nMMAudio\nA solitary horse gallops across the vast plain, its thundering hooves pounding the earth and sending sharp \nsnorts of wind through the air. Its mane and tail flutter in the breeze.\nOurs\nMMAudio\nA person is using an ice axe to tap on a large chunk of ice on a smooth surface, exposing its crystalline \nstructure.\nFigure 33: Audio generation samples of MMAudio and ours.\n46\n\n\n--- PAGE 47 ---\nImplementation details. Our V2A architecture generates high-fidelity stereo audio with a maximum\nduration of 12 seconds at a 44.1 kHz sampling rate. The model processes multimodal inputs through\ndedicated encoders: umT5-XXL produces 4096-dimensional text embeddings, while the CLIP\nextracts 1024-dimensional visual embeddings per frame. The audio and video features are projected\ninto a unified 1536-dimensional latent space within the DiT backbone. For temporal alignment, we\ndownsample input videos and yield precisely 48 frames for 12-second clips, ensuring frame-accurate\nsynchronization between visual and audio modalities. The system processes latent sequences of\nlength 256 in one batch. To enhance the model’s capability of generating audio solely from visual\ncues, we implement a random masking strategy during training where ambient sound and music\ncaptions are selectively omitted with a predefined probability. This conditioning mechanism forces\nthe model to establish robust cross-modal associations between visual content and corresponding\naudio patterns, while maintaining the flexibility to utilize textual descriptions when available.\n5.7.2\nEVALUATION\nWe provide qualitative evaluations of audio generation in Fig. 33, where we compare our method\nwith the recently released open-sourced approach MMAudio (Cheng et al., 2024). This comparative\nanalysis is particularly meaningful as MMAudio represents the most similar and competitive baseline\nto our method, demonstrating impressive performance in audio generation tasks. The videos are\ngenerated by our text-to-video model with a duration of 5 seconds.\nOur V2A model demonstrates superior performance in several key aspects of audio generation, as\nevidenced by the comparative results. Specifically, the model exhibits enhanced long-term consistency,\nparticularly noticeable in the ‘pouring coffee’ scenario (the first case in Fig. 33). In the second case\nof ‘typing’, our approach generates significantly cleaner audio output compared to the noisier results\nproduced by MMAudio. Furthermore, our method excels in synthesizing rhythmic sound patterns, as\ndemonstrated in the ‘walking’, ‘boxing’, and ‘clip-clop’ examples (the last three cases in Fig. 33),\nwhere it achieves more natural and coherent audio generation.\nLimitations. Our method exhibits limitations in generating human vocal sounds, including but not\nlimited to laughter, crying, and speech. This limitation is primarily attributed to our data preparation\nprocess, where speech-related vocal data is deliberately excluded from the training dataset. The\nMMAudio model’s ability to produce random speech-like sounds stems from its retention of speech-\nrelated data in its training corpus. In our future work, we plan to incorporate speech generation\ncapabilities to address this limitation.\n6\nLIMITATION AND CONCLUSION\nLimitation. This study presents Wan, a foundational video generation model that has achieved notable\nadvancements across multiple benchmarks. Specifically, significant improvements have been observed\nin motion amplitude (e.g., sports, dance) and instruction-following capabilities. Nevertheless, several\nlimitations persist, which should be addressed to realize more potential of Wan. First, preserving\nfine-grained details in scenarios involving large motion remains a challenge for our method, as well\nas a broader issue within the field of video generation. Substantial efforts are required to improve the\nfidelity of videos with large-scale motion dynamics. Second, the computational cost associated with\nlarge-scale models remains prohibitive. Inference on a 14B model currently requires approximately 30\nminutes on a single head-end GPU without additional optimization. To democratize video generation\nas a universally accessible AI tool, further research into efficiency and scalability is essential. Finally,\nthere is still a lack of domain-specific expertise. As a foundational video model, we are committed\nto enhancing Wan’s general capabilities, yet performance in specific localized scenarios, such as\neducation and medicine, may be insufficient. We aim to address this limitation by open-sourcing our\nlatest models and fostering community-driven development across diverse specialized domains.\nConclusion. In this report, we publicly release Wan, our latest video model, which establishes a\nnew benchmark for video generation. We provide a comprehensive overview of the architectural\ndesign of our Wan-VAE and DiT models, including detailed insights into their training approach,\ndata curation processes, evaluation methods, and empirical results. Furthermore, we meticulously\nanalyze our data preprocessing pipeline and the strategic adaptations implemented to optimize model\ntraining. This holistic approach is designed to drive advancements in the domain of video generation.\nWe also extensively investigate downstream applications, such as image-to-video generation, video\n47\n\n\n--- PAGE 48 ---\nediting, and personalized video generation, to demonstrate the versatility and practical utility of Wan\nacross diverse scenarios. In addition to open-sourcing the 14B model, we explore the feasibility\nof leveraging smaller-scale models for efficient video generation. Notably, our 1.3B model not\nonly achieves competitive performance compared to larger counterparts but also enables seamless\ninference on consumer-grade GPUs, significantly enhancing accessibility and practicality for content\ncreators. Looking ahead, we plan to focus on scaling both data and model architectures to tackle\nthe most pressing challenges in video generation. Our ongoing efforts aim to provide the research\ncommunity with more robust and versatile tools for video creation, fostering innovation and broader\nadoption in this rapidly evolving field.\n48\n\n\n--- PAGE 49 ---\n7\nCONTRIBUTORS\nAuthors are listed alphabetically by the first name.\nAng Wang\nBaole Ai\nBin Wen\nChaojie Mao\nChen-Wei Xie\nDi Chen\nFeiwu Yu\nHaiming Zhao\nJianxiao Yang\nJianyuan Zeng\nJiayu Wang\nJingfeng Zhang\nJingren Zhou\nJinkai Wang\nJixuan Chen\nKai Zhu\nKang Zhao\nKeyu Yan\nLianghua Huang\nMengyang Feng\nNingyi Zhang\nPandeng Li\nPingyu Wu\nRuihang Chu\nRuili Feng\nShiwei Zhang\nSiyang Sun\nTao Fang\nTianxing Wang\nTianyi Gui\nTingyu Weng\nTong Shen\nWei Lin\nWei Wang˜1\nWei Wang˜2\nWenmeng Zhou\nWente Wang\nWenting Shen\nWenyuan Yu\nXianzhong Shi\nXiaoming Huang\nXin Xu\nYan Kou\nYangyu Lv\nYifei Li\nYijing Liu\nYiming Wang\nYingya Zhang\nYitong Huang\nYong Li\nYou Wu\nYu Liu\nYulin Pan\nYun Zheng\nYuntao Hong\nYupeng Shi\nYutong Feng\nZeyinzi Jiang\nZhen Han\nZhi-Fan Wu\nZiyu Liu\n49\n\n\n--- PAGE 50 ---\nREFERENCES\nRunway AI.
